The former Fulham full-back held the record for being the Premier League's youngest-ever player for 12 years

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Matthew Briggs became an instant pub quiz question in his very fist game in senior football.

By coming off the bench against Middlesbrough in May 2007, the Fulham left-back set a new Premier League record as its youngest-ever player.

Briggs inherited the distinction from Izzy Brown and would give it up to Harvey Elliott 12 years later, before Ethan Nwaneri set the mark at 15 years, 181 days in 2022.

But as Briggs told FourFourTwo: "Being the Premier League’s youngest player was one of the greatest moments and achievements of my life, but it came with a lot of pressure.

"When I eventually lost the title to Harvey Elliott after 12 long years - the longest anyone has had it - it felt like the pressure had been lifted and I could just be me.

"People had always been wondering why I wasn’t doing this and that, why I wasn’t achieving what they believed I should have been achieving.

"It’s a lot of noise to deal with. I have sympathy for Harvey Elliott now, too.

The best features, fun and footballing quizzes, straight to your inbox every week.

I actually thought he was doing well at Liverpool, and he’s still young enough to get back to doing what he does best."

Briggs believes that starting at such a high level so young can make the adaptation that much harder if you end up having to work your way back up the pyramid.

That's particularly true if your first move away comes as abruptly as it did for Briggs when he left Fulham for Millwall in 2014in the first permanent switch of his itinerant career.

Briggs explained: "I suffered a lot of injuries, although I was offered a three-year contract extension before I left Fulham.

"But Martin Jol got sacked, a new manager came in and got rid of the whole team – I was injured at the time, and he wanted to bring in fresh blood. I’d been there since the age of 10.

"When you drop down the leagues, you have the aspiration to get back to the top, but when you get to a certain age, it’s harder to do that, especially when you’re suffering with injuries.

"I suffered a lot with mental health because of that. Everything that was my superpower – speed, power, getting up and down the pitch, my body was stopping me from doing it.

"I went to Denmark and really enjoyed my first season, but then my hip started playing up again and they told me I should retire, because I had no cartilage in my hip. I came back to the UK and played non-league for two more years, but my body just gave up."

Steven Chicken has been working as a football writer since 2009, taking in stints with Football365 and the Huddersfield Examiner. Steven still covers Huddersfield Town home and away for his own publication, WeAreTerriers.com. Steven is a two-time nominee for Regional Journalist of the Year at the prestigious British Sports Journalism Awards, making the shortlist in 2020 and 2023.

Lisa Lennon was born on the Isle of Man, a place steeped in myth and folklore. That perhaps shows in her art style, which defines as magical realism with a focus on classical portraiture. She works with watercolours, building up many layers while adding details.

Below, Lisa introduces four examples of her work. If you're inspired and need supplies for your own art, see our guides to the best watercolour paints and the best watercolour pencils.

“This science fiction piece was dreamed up while walking by the sea. Her headpiece is inspired by the pebbles there. I love wide open spaces; they give me a lot of scope to play with atmospheric scenes.”

“This is where fantasy meets science fiction. I was working with local folklore and became obsessed with goblins and elves. I developed a collection called ‘Space Goblins’.”

“Another in the ‘Space Goblins’ series. How did Tudor clothes end up in outer space? We can only speculate, but I do love a good riff, and it’s always a large technical challenge to get it right.”

“One of my classic goblins. I so enjoy painting these rather cantankerous, sassy personalities. My focus here – technique-wise – was to have a vintage look to the colour and quality, all while retaining a contemporary, whimsical feel. I love this one.”

The Sky Today on Thursday, November 19: Mercury moves into Libra

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Looking for a sky event this week? Check out our full Sky This Week column.

November 18: The Leonid meteor shower peaks

The solar system is a dynamic place. Let’s return to the predawn sky today to check out the inner planets’ progress relative to the stars of Virgo. An hour before sunrise, both Mercury and Venus are again well above the eastern horizon, with the stars around them still visible before twilight brightens too much.

Compared with earlier in the week, Venus now sits to the upper left of Spica, rather than directly above it. Venus will pass 1.8° due north of Spica later today at 6 P.M. EST. Now moving steadily east, it will track along the ecliptic, slowly headed for Libra. Mercury has already reached the Balance, sitting within its domain and to the far lower left of Kappa (κ) Virginis, as opposed to the pair’s close proximity a few days ago.

Pull out your telescope to see how the planets’ appearances have changed, too. Mercury is now 55% lit and 7” across, while Venus is 18% lit and has shrunk more noticeably, now 47” across.

Sunrise: 6:50 A.M.Sunset: 4:40 P.M.Moonrise: 1:41 P.M.Moonset: 1:07 A.M.Moon Phase: Waxing gibbous (76%)*Times for sunrise, sunset, moonrise, and moonset are given in local time from 40° N 90° W. The Moon’s illumination is given at 10 P.M. local time from the same location.

Alison Klesman is senior editor of Astronomy magazine. She holds a Ph.D. in astronomy and has studied a variety of topics, from minor planets to supermassive black holes.

InfoQ Homepage News NetWasm 0.10 Speeds Up C# on WebAssembly, Now Faster Than Mono AOT

Nov 19, 2026 3 min read

The team behind NetWasm has published a write-up of its 0.10 optimisation work, spanning releases 0.10.1 to 0.10.8. The changes touch exception handling, array bounds checks, WebAssembly SIMD and integer division. On the project’s own benchmark set, NetWasm now comes out ahead of Mono AOT and gets close to desktop .NET on some workloads.

NetWasm is a compiler and runtime that compiles C# programs ahead of time into small, standalone WebAssembly files. It takes the IL that Roslyn produces and emits Wasm, using its own runtime and a CoreLib ported from .NET. The yardstick is the Mono AOT build for WASI, which runs .NET on the same engine. A new garbage collector had already put NetWasm in front on allocation-heavy code, but plain loops were still slower: a quicksort of 100,000 integers took 77.2 ms, against 58.0 ms for Mono AOT.

Much of the early gain came from exception handling in 0.10.1. Wasmtime adds extra register save-and-restore code around any function that can throw or catch, floating-point registers included. Methods with null, bounds or checked-arithmetic checks paid that price on every call. Throws now go through a shared function and the catching handler releases the frames, which cut UI churn from 17.6 to 7.9 ms.

Version 0.10.2 inlines small straight-line methods in loops, tuple swaps included, and skips checks already performed on the same array and index. The quicksort fell to 54.0 ms. DoomSharp, a C# software renderer for Doom that ships a NetWasm build, served as a second test case. Value-type accessors now compile to plain field loads, and at O3 small branching helpers are inlined inside loops. Together these brought a 120-frame render from 3,595 down to 1,934 ms.

SIMD arrived in 0.10.7. Vector128, Vector and PackedSimd lower to 128-bit Wasm instructions, and element-wise array loops handle four or more elements per iteration, with a scalar fallback. Hoisting loop-invariant Math calls allowed a solver for Burgers’ equation, a fundamental partial differential equation from fluid mechanics, to vectorise: its first test dropped from 497 ms to 71 ms, against 186 ms for Mono AOT and 93 ms for desktop .NET.

Version 0.10.8 targets division and range-checked arithmetic. A remainder that follows a division now reuses the quotient and skips repeated zero-divisor and int.MinValue / -1 checks. A trial-division test fell from 1,376 to 765 ms, while Mono AOT needed 862 ms.

Across the 2,213 cases, 0.10.8 at O3 with NetWasmGC takes 0.41 times Mono AOT’s time as a geometric mean, excluding five cases where Mono’s LLVM removes the work. Against desktop .NET 11 RC3 JIT the ratio is 0.87, down from 2.15 for 0.10.6. By family, the ratio to Mono AOT runs from 0.34 for intrinsics to 0.81 for math. The 120-frame DoomSharp render takes 1,623 ms on NetWasmGC, compared with 2,371 ms for Mono AOT and 1,053 ms for desktop .NET JIT.

Those figures come from Wasmtime 49.0.3 on an Apple M1 Max, using cases from dotnet/performance plus five NetWasm workloads. The authors also found that identical loops at different addresses could differ by up to 15 per cent, so their A/B tests align every function to 4 KiB.

The article is open about what is left. Desktop .NET is about 3.1 times faster on vector benchmarks, because it can use host SIMD directly while Wasm is limited to fixed 128-bit operations. Math.Pow is 1.4 times slower than on Mono AOT, and CopyBlock64 is 3.3 times slower. One workload, NewtR, regressed to 1.9 times slower than in 0.10.6, which the authors blame on Wasmtime’s register allocator keeping a value in a stack slot around the loop.

Since the numbers are self-reported and come from one machine and one runtime, results elsewhere may differ. NetWasm can be tried in the online playground, and local setup is covered in the compile and publish guide. Developers can find the documentation is in the official GitHub repository.

Presented by: David Iparraguirre - Senior Product Marketing Manager at Datadog, and Jessica Yang - Product Manager at Datadog

Could being bored save your creative career?

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

A couple of weeks ago, it was my 43rd birthday, and I spent a good few days doing absolutely nothing. My wife, Kate, knows me really well, and freed up my long weekend so I could mooch around as I pleased.

I went for long walks by myself, we went for short walks together, we ate, danced, cuddled on the sofa a little with our son . . . you know, the good stuff. A younger version of me might have thought that sounded incredibly boring.

These days, it’s exactly how I want to spend my time.

And it’s got me thinking about that b-word: boredom.

I believe this particular b-word has a bad reputation. Especially in the world of business, freelance, and entrepreneurship, where we are continuously being encouraged to hustle and fill all of our waking time with moneymaking activities. Keep learning, keep posting, just so you can keep the lights on.

Today, we’re surrounded by more creative tools, access to inspiration, and potential new opportunities than at any other point in history, but I wonder whether all this stimulation is coming at a cost.

And the more I think about this, the more I wonder whether we are becoming so good at distracting ourselves that we’re forgetting how to think for ourselves. 

Have we become allergic to doing nothing?

I often find myself in random little moments trying to fill the empty space.

Waiting for a bus? Pick up your phone.

Standing in a queue? Pick up your phone.

Going to the bathroom? Best take your phone.

As a species of humans, we’ve become incredibly good at filling every available second with . . . something. And it’s not all our fault. We have these little machines with incredibly distracting toys on them that have been built to make distraction convenient.

Perhaps more so than many others, the creative industry is among the most susceptible to the stimulation machine. Jobs and roles rely on us being inspired and on top of trends and other fluffery.

We design and create for others, and need to check and develop with devices and humans in mind. I don’t know the stats, but I would hazard a guess that due to our industry, we tend to use the tools, apps, and devices more on a daily basis than anyone else. I would hate to think how long I personally spend looking at a screen of one sort or another every day.

But something happens when you spend your time looking at a device that continuously shares the thoughts and opinions of others. You start to think the same, envy what you don’t have yet, and struggle to think for yourself. 

Over my 20 years in this industry, I’ve learned that the most important part of being a designer isn’t how quickly I can make something, although the bros on the internet seem rather adamant that I’m soon to be cooked.

It’s the deliberate nature of how I think about what I’m making.

Thinking requires something we’re becoming increasingly uncomfortable with.

Silence.

What did our brains do before smartphones?

When I work, I often sit in silence.

When I drive, I often sit in silence. 

When I go for a walk, sometimes for four, five, or even eight hours, I like silence. And when I’m on a train, I sit and stare out of the window, usually looking at all the other people looking at their phones.

I am an exciting chap, I know.

But I find these quiet moments immensely valuable.

A few years ago, I came across the work of Harvard professor Arthur C. Brooks, who got me intrigued about what happens when we give our brains a little extra room to do their own thing.

There’s some fascinating, geeky neuroscience behind this stuff that I’ve enjoyed learning about.

Scientists have identified something called the default mode network, or DMN. It’s a collection of interconnected brain regions associated with internally directed thinking—you know, remembering things, imagining possible futures, reflecting on your past experiences, daydreaming, and all that stuff you do in the shower.

Sounds a little like Narnia, doesn’t it?

Interestingly, the DMN can only switch on when you’re not focused on external tasks.

A 2024 study published in the journal Brain provided further evidence that this network plays an important role in creative thinking. Researchers recorded brain activity during spontaneous and divergent thinking tasks, finding evidence that the default mode network contributes to creative idea generation.

Now, that doesn’t mean sitting around doing nothing will magically turn you into a creative genius. If only that were true. But it does suggest that some of the mental processes involved in creativity happen when we allow our attention to move away from the task directly in front of us.

Maybe there is a weird little irony in the fact that sometimes the best way to think about something is to stop actively thinking about it.

Maybe there is actual science behind ‘I get my ideas in the shower’

Surely we have all heard someone say this at least once. I know Albert Einstein was famous for figuring out complex problems on the long walks he took daily.

Monotonous tasks like washing the dishes, walking the dog, doing the laundry, or even mowing the lawn have all sparked brilliant business ideas for people. So while doing a little more digging, I learned about a study involving something called creative incubation.

Participants in the study were given a set of creative problems. Then they were given different activities to do before returning to those problems.

A funny thing occurred.

The people who took a break to do something simple came back with better ideas. They even outperformed those who had taken a full-on rest or spent their break doing something more demanding. The researchers concluded that giving our minds a chance to wander could actually help us think more creatively.

Also, Stanford researchers have found that walking increases creative output by an average of 60% compared with sitting. The meaningless movement or task is what allows our DMN to perform for us. Essentially, when we wander our minds are free to wonder.

Perhaps doing nothing is exactly what we need to do

I think there’s something here for all of us, somewhat hidden among the shower thoughts and hum of boredom. Whether we’re designers, artists, entrepreneurs, business owners, or whatever we choose to call ourselves, we now participate in a world that’s continuously telling us to do more, share more, make more, and be more productive. It’s conditioned us to see every empty moment as another opportunity to fill our brains with stuff.

But perhaps it’s a trap.

Maybe the long walks without headphones, the train journeys without our smartphones, and the quiet weekends with our families are exactly what we need more of. The nothingness. The meandering. The pensive stimulation of being ever-present in any particular moment.

In our rush to be productive, we’re actually limiting our brain’s capacity; we’re getting in the way of it doing its much-needed “hoovering”: to daydream, reflect, question things, and imagine what could be one day.

Now, I am no creative genius, but I wonder whether the lack of originality we see in the creative industry today has something to do with how much time we spend consuming other people’s thoughts, on a little black device, rather than giving ourselves the opportunity to develop our own.

Maybe boredom will help you think about your next move.

Maybe it will help you reflect on how awesome you actually are.

And maybe, just maybe, that next long walk in silence will change your life.

Ellipsis®: Strategy Lead (Remote, UK/EU -- £60-65k)

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Ellipsis is the market leader in AI search for B2B technology companies, on a mission to be the best in the world. We make B2B technology companies most-recommended by AI (ChatGPT and Google). Our 15-person remote team combines ambition with real results.

Ellipsis is fast becoming the market leader in "GEO" for B2B technology companies. This is a senior, hands-on role in a fast-moving and ambitious agency.

The move to AI search has put a lot of the SEO industry on the back foot. Ellipsis has always been consultative and strategic in our approach. We started building AI search products in 2021 on GPT-2, so this has been a time for us to thrive.

We think we understand AI search better than almost anyone. We have our own tracking product, ~25 million AI answers to analyze (with more tracked every week), and execution end-to-end: strategy, writing, in-house design, optimization, and performance reporting.

Marketing teams everywhere are working out what AI search means for them, and we're one of the few firms with credible answers backed by work we've already done. That's taking us beyond B2B tech scale-ups into conversations with global retailers, banks, and consumer brands, and into work well beyond search: positioning, websites, and how buyers choose between suppliers.

As Strategy Lead, you'll decide what work we do for every client, and why, and shape how our latest research on AI Search feeds into this decision. You'll run the Strategy team, set the strategy for our biggest accounts yourself, and build a strategy service that holds its quality as we take on much larger projects.

You'll own strategy across Ellipsis: what we recommend for each client, in what order, and why. You’ll ensure that all work we do for clients is adapted based on our constantly growing understanding of AI Search. You'll report directly to the MD/Founder and sit on the leadership team alongside the Accounts Lead, Head of Operations, and Chief of Staff.

Ellipsis is at the forefront of demand as SEO turns into GEO. We are growing very quickly as buyers are deciding now whom to trust with AI search, and some are bringing us in at board level as part of their AI strategy. The firms that earn those relationships over the next year will be hard to replace.

Our view is that AI makes yesterday's expertise cheap, so the job is to keep producing today's expertise and applying it well. Every client project should teach us more about what works, and that learning should make the next project faster and better. You’ll work closely with our Research and Development analyst to shape overall strategy, work with the team to ensure this is effectively used in practice, and ensure we have an effective feedback loop of findings <> implementation <> upskilling the team to ask better questions <> identifying gaps and new research.

Your job is to provide strategic judgement and ensure work gets done across the whole team. We have more data, tooling and ideas than any single client needs! And so your role will be identifying the leverage points across our customers; what can be done that makes performance better across the board? How can we deliver more of what our clients need? How can we build or improve automations that help us deliver more value? How do we apply findings, data, and research that actually lands?

You will own a couple of (high level or at risk – depending where you are most needed) clients and will need to be comfortable presenting, answering hard questions from clients, and building trust with our customers. You’ll need to be doing client work in order to understand how we do Strategy, what works, etc.; but client work is a max of 50% of the role.

You will work closely with other senior members of the team: our Accounts Lead owns client relationships, and you will need to collaborate closely; our Head of Operations owns capacity and process; our Founder&MD leads the business and is hands-on in the weeds with clients; and we’re currently hiring a Research and Development analyst to own the research (currently this is split across the team). You’ll also need to work closely with our Writing team and Content team to ensure questions are answered, strategy is understood, and everyone is doing the right work!

You will lead and line manage our Strategy team (currently 3); their personal development and progression is very important and you will have the chance to shape their up-skilling and growth! Search is changing quickly, and you’ll help our ambitious team progress rapidly.

We want to see progress in the key metrics we and our clients care about across the board – clients recommended, and picked, more often on the buying questions that matter to them, and that showing up in their pipeline. Alongside it, we want to see progress in the whole Strategy team's output: more clients and larger programmes carried by the same people, without quality slipping, and progress / progression for the team members too. Our understanding of AI Search continues to develop, and you’ll be instrumental in ensuring the work we do for clients adapts quickly to our latest research.

You'll see it working day to day! Every client has a plan they can explain in a sentence! Work lands when we said it would! Strategists spend most of their week doing strategy instead of preparing for meetings about it. And client calls never need the founder to step in to answer a question or rescue a number.

Decide the work for every client. You own the plan for each account: what we do month on month, what we don't, and why. We have far more data and tooling than any one client needs, so most of your value is in choosing / discerning the two or three things that will move the result and saying no to the rest. You’ll need to be confident in understanding industry shifts and our latest research, and feeding that into the work we do for clients.

Make strategy simple. Turn complicated analysis into a recommendation a client, a Content Manager or a writer can act on first time, without coming back to you. Lead with the answer, and caveat only when it changes the decision. A one-page plan the client agrees with beats a 40-slide deck they have to decode.

Front strategy on the biggest accounts. You set the strategy for a handful of our largest, most complex accounts yourself and present it on calls with the Accounts Lead. You know the numbers, you answer the hard questions in the room, and can confidently and clearly present to senior stakeholders.

You'll need to be effective on these calls from your first few weeks. As the team grows, you'll do less of this day-to-day, but you'll still step in confidently whenever a client needs you.

Build strategy that scales to enterprise. Our newer projects span several markets and, in some cases, several brands. You design strategy that holds up across all of them: one approach per portfolio, adapted locally, with quality checks that don't depend on one senior person reading everything.

Take strategy closer to the sale. Clients increasingly bring us into positioning, website decisions and commercial performance reviews. Point our strategy at the decisions that move pipeline, using what buyers ask AI and what clients' sales teams hear to shape what clients say about themselves.

Run the Strategy team. You lead and develop a small, growing team of strategists, and upskill them so they can handle accounts at your level. You review their plans before clients see them, coach their judgment, and protect their time. You make it easy for them to ask for help early, and you notice when they haven't. Their progression is extremely important.

Plan to capacity. Our tooling generates more good ideas than any team can deliver. You make sure the plan fits the hours, working with Operations so what we promise each client is what Content, Writing and Design can ship on time. When something has to slip, you decide what and say so early.

Own our playbook and how we measure it. AI search changes weekly. You decide which new findings change how we work, update the playbook, and get the team using it. You also work with our Account Lead to define success for clients -- which questions count, what counts as a recommendation, how we weight them -- so every number in a client report means something commercially.

Shape what our tooling and agents do. Ellipsis builds its own AI search technology, and more of our delivery is moving to agents. You decide which strategy work they take on, check their output is right, and feed that back to our tech team, who build it. They own the tooling; you shape what it does for strategy.

Back up sales. Give our sales team a sharp strategic view on prospects quickly: what they need and what we'd do first. A clear view early in the process is often what wins the work.

You've led strategy for a portfolio of client accounts and managed strategists doing the same. In UK agencies this is typically a Head of SEO, SEO Director or Strategy Director, with 6-10 years in organic search and at least two of those managing people. Experience with enterprise or multi-market clients is a strong advantage. We're open to other routes if you can show the judgment.

You'll need a strong grasp of organic search fundamentals and a working understanding of AI search. We'll teach you how we think about AI search; we can't teach judgment. That means making a clear call from imperfect data, defending it calmly, and changing it when the evidence says so.

This is very much a client facing role. You will be expected to interact with the clients in tandem with the Accounts Lead regularly in meetings. Having experience handling strategic conversations, managing high level discussions and presentations with clients, from junior to C-suite is essential to your success.

You simplify. You can explain a client's strategy in one sentence and defend it for ten minutes. Your decks got shorter as you got more senior.

Your priorities hold. You decide what matters this week, it stays decided, and when something will slip you say so before anyone has to ask.

You take feedback without ego. When you're wrong, you change course. At Ellipsis, continuous improvement matters more than being right! Especially as so much is changing now.

You use AI for speed, not slop or sprawl. You use Claude or ChatGPT daily, and you know the difference between work AI made faster and work AI just made bigger.

You want to drive things forward. You'd rather design a strategy service that works for 50 clients than be the hero on five.

You're a doer. You'd rather get the right work live for a client than spend a week building a tool to do it, and you're at your best in the room, with clients and with your team.

Shadow the Founder and the Accounts Lead on the main accounts, review strategy, data, etc and see how we work; and meet each strategist one-to-one. Get up to speed over the first month, and be effective on client calls for those accounts from early on.

By day 60, you're presenting strategy on those accounts, and every client plan is sized against the team's real capacity. By day 90, you own client strategy across the business, plans land on time, you've started building a way of working that lets the team carry more clients, strategists need you less on their accounts, and the founder joins strategy calls by exception only.

By six months, you're hitting your stride: adapting the work we do for clients as our understanding of AI Search changes, with less of your week spent on day-to-day client strategy.

Singapore settles $77.5M tokenized bill sale with wholesale CBDC

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

MAS issued the bills directly on its test network, which it plans to rename SG Digital Ledger and use for settlement beyond the trials.

The Monetary Authority of Singapore (MAS) issued 100 million Singapore dollars ($77.5 million) in tokenized bills on Nov. 6, with banks using wholesale central bank digital currency (CBDC) to pay for them.

Five banks submitted bids totaling more than twice the amount on offer, MAS said Thursday. The four-week bills mature on Dec. 4.

MAS issued the bills directly on the Singapore Dollar Test Network. That gives borrowers an asset they could pledge as collateral for loans on the network, the central bank said.

Singapore began experimenting with blockchain-based bank payments in 2016. In 2025, banks used the test network and MAS-issued digital currency to settle overnight loans.

MAS now plans to turn the Singapore Dollar Test Network, where the bills were issued, into a settlement system for financial institutions, Managing Director Chia Der Jiun said Thursday at the Singapore FinTech Festival. The network will be renamed SG Digital Ledger.

“As the use of tokenized assets and digital money grows, trusted settlement on CBDC will be needed,” Chia said. “It underpins settlement finality and confidence, especially for large value settlement.”

In 2027, MAS plans to issue more tokenized bills, bring more financial institutions into the trials and expand the bills’ use as collateral.

Germany and Austria call for TikTok’s EU business to go to European owners

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The two governments cite data access by the Chinese state and want other EU capitals to back the plan.

Germany and Austria want TikTok’s European business to come under European ownership. Ministers from both countries made the call in a joint statement on Wednesday, POLITICO reported.

Germany’s media minister Wolfram Weimer and Austria’s state secretary for digitalisation Alexander Pröll said the move would keep personal data safe and give Europe more control. They called TikTok a threat to democracy.

The problem starts with TikTok’s Chinese owner ByteDance, the ministers said. They fear the Chinese state can get at the data of millions of Europeans.

TikTok has about 200 million users in the EU, according to the statement. The ministers called on other EU countries to join them, but did not say how a new owner would be found.

TikTok rejected the idea, POLITICO reported.

A TikTok spokesperson said there was “” to the proposal.

The plan follows the US model. In January, TikTok’s US business moved into a new joint venture led by Oracle, Silver Lake and MGX. ByteDance kept a stake of nearly 20%.

Weimer first pushed the idea in May. At the time, the European Commission said it looks at compliance with EU rules, not at who owns a company.

TikTok is already under pressure in Europe. Ireland’s data regulator fined TikTok €530m in 2025 over transfers of European user data to China. The EU is also looking into its role in elections.

The Paradox of American Credibility

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Since its founding in 1922, Foreign Affairs has been the leading forum for serious discussion of American foreign policy and global affairs. The magazine has featured contributions from many leading international affairs experts.

JOSHUA A. SCHWARTZ is Assistant Professor of International Relations and Emerging Technology at the Carnegie Mellon Institute for Strategy & Technology.

How Fighting Weakens Washington’s Reputation—and Backing Down May Strengthen It

In mid-February, after the United States failed to get Iran to renounce its nuclear ambitions, U.S. President Donald Trump issued an ultimatum. The Iranian leadership had to make “a meaningful deal” with Washington. “Otherwise,” he said, “bad things happen.” Barely eight days later, the United States and Israel began the war against Iran that continues today.

That war has been criticized on many grounds, but analysts have often overlooked the basic assumption about U.S. credibility that it has tested. There is a long-held understanding that the United States’ willingness to use force to stand up for its interests is essential to deterring future adversaries—and convincing them to give in to the kind of sweeping demands the Trump administration made of Tehran. As Elbridge Colby, U.S. undersecretary of defense for policy, said in the early stages of the conflict, “I think both our friends and potential foes are seeing the prowess of the joint force and that also contributes to deterrence.”

Going back to the early years of the Cold War, the concept of credibility—a state’s perceived capabilities and its willingness to use them even at high cost—has played a central role in the decisions that led to and sustained many of the United States’ largest military campaigns. The need to bolster U.S. credibility was a key force driving Washington’s strategy in Korea and Vietnam in the mid-twentieth century, and it played an important role in Iraq and Afghanistan during the so-called war on terror. American strategists today continue to assess foreign policy decisions based on how they might strengthen or weaken the country’s reputation.

But as the results of the Iran campaign have shown, a decision to use force does not always strengthen deterrence. After nearly ten months of war, and the expenditure of billions of dollars of munitions, the United States appears little closer to achieving its objectives of definitively ending Iran’s nuclear threat and ability to project power across the region, let alone securing regime change in Tehran. Meanwhile, the war has badly depleted U.S. military resources that could be used elsewhere and has further eroded the American public’s willingness to support military interventions. Rather than sending a strong signal of U.S. resolve, the Iran war risks conveying the opposite message. Vice President JD Vance argued that the reason the Republicans lost the midterm elections was the Iran war, and government officials in Tehran have gloated that “Americans said no to war.”

In fact, the conventional wisdom that standing firm in a military conflict enhances a state’s credibility misses something important. As the U.S. wars in Vietnam and Iraq showed, a prolonged military campaign may leave the country more cautious about using force, and less willing to bear the costs, in the years that follow. Adversaries notice this. Given that prior efforts by the Trump administration to compromise with Tehran were criticized as “appeasement” by figures like former U.S. Vice President Mike Pence, Trump may be tempted to continue or even expand the Iran campaign. But by taxing U.S. capabilities and deepening war-weariness among the American public, escalation in Iran could instead lead Beijing and Moscow to question Washington’s appetite for future interventions.

Policymakers should be far more skeptical about using military force for the sake of demonstrating U.S. resolve, and they should be less averse to backing down. The Cold War competition with the Soviet Union convinced the United States to intervene in costly proxy wars that consumed enormous human and military resources for questionable gain, and now the strategic competition with China and Russia may do the same. These kinds of conflicts are precisely the ones that are likely to harm, rather than help, U.S. credibility. Instead of obsessing about its reputation for resolve, which it cannot control, Washington must refocus its attention on maintaining the military strength and capabilities it needs to deter its major adversaries. Sometimes the best way to preserve both the capability and the willingness to act in the future is to walk away from conflict in the present.

For decades, policymakers and scholars have treated a country’s credibility as the dark matter of international politics—a hard-to-measure attribute that is nonetheless enormously important for maintaining deterrence. In the 1960s, nuclear deterrence theorist and Nobel Prize winner Thomas Schelling went so far as to say that acquiring a reputation for resolve is “one of the few things worth fighting over.” In keeping with this, it has long been assumed that whereas standing firm communicates to adversaries and allies alike that a country is willing to run risks and bear costs to uphold its interests, backing down or seeking to compromise conveys the opposite. As a result, many U.S. leaders and strategists have drawn on the logic of credibility to justify the most hawkish policies toward adversaries: making maximalist demands, using force, and remaining in wars long past the point where their costs exceeded their benefits.

Vietnam was a case in point. When debating whether to send the first group of U.S. troops to Vietnam in 1965, U.S. Secretary of State Dean Rusk told President Lyndon Johnson, “If the Communist world finds out we will not pursue our commitments to the end, I don’t know where they will stay their hand.” Later, as the United States became bogged down in the war, Assistant Secretary of Defense John McNaughton went further, arguing in a formerly top-secret memo that the primary reason for continuing the Vietnam War was to maintain America’s “reputation as a guarantor” and “avoid a humiliating U.S. defeat.” According to this logic, 58,000 U.S. soldiers and likely over a million Vietnamese people were killed not because the outcome of the war was itself crucial to U.S. interests, but because Washington worried how others—especially the Soviets—would perceive it if it withdrew.

Since the Cold War, the same justifications have been used to push the country into, and to sustain, many other conflicts. This was especially true during the so-called Forever Wars the United States was drawn into after the 9/11 attacks. On the night of 9/11, U.S. Secretary of Defense Donald Rumsfeld said, “We need to bomb something else [other than the relatively weak Taliban] to prove that we’re, you know, big and strong and not going to be pushed around by these kinds of attacks.” The logic of credibility not only played a role in Washington launching the Iraq war in 2003 but also locked it into the conflict, even as the costs rose and the raison d’être for the war—weapons of mass destruction—turned out to be false. As President George W. Bush said in 2006, “If we were to leave before the mission is complete, it would hurt U.S. credibility.”

Conversely, strategists have been quick to criticize U.S. leaders who backed down. During the Syrian civil war, many were concerned about the reputational damage to the United States when President Barack Obama declined to respond to the Syrian government’s use of sarin gas to attack a rebel-held suburb of Damascus, thereby violating a red line that Obama had previously declared. In 2021, many foreign policy hawks reprimanded President Joe Biden for withdrawing from Afghanistan and for waiving some U.S. sanctions on Russia’s Nord Stream 2 pipeline project. At the time, Michael McCaul, the top Republican on the House Foreign Affairs Committee, said, “U.S. credibility from Kyiv to Taipei cannot withstand another blow of this nature.” More recently, Trump himself has been accused of backing down against China on tariffs, which some have decried as another example of “Trump always chickens out,” or “TACO.” In each of these cases, critics argued that Washington’s failure to act has compromised the perception that the United States is willing to run risks and bear costs to achieve its objectives.

Despite the persistent assumption that U.S. credibility requires using force and refusing to back down, that thinking has been repeatedly challenged over the past decade. For one thing, experience has shown that a country’s past behavior may be a poor indicator of its present-day resolve. In April, for example, The New York Times reported that Trump believed that Iran was unlikely to attempt to close the Strait of Hormuz based on its past actions—such as its failure to mount a robust response to the U.S. and Israeli bombing of its nuclear facilities in the 12-day war in 2025. In reality, however, Iran has been far less restrained than expected in the current conflict and moved quickly not only to close the strait but also to attack U.S. allies and U.S. military bases across the region. In other words, Tehran’s failure to stand firm in 2025 had little bearing on its resolve in the present war.

In any case, it is far from clear that a country’s reputation for resolve is something that it can control by its actions alone. As Keren Yarhi-Milo has explained in Foreign Affairs, “credibility is in the eye of the beholder.” Even if a state acts decisively to signal strength, an adversary may not interpret that action as reason to reconsider its behavior. For example, despite Washington’s intention to communicate high resolve by bearing tremendous costs in the Vietnam War, the North Vietnamese Communist Party Central Committee concluded in 1965 that “the domestic situation in the United States, however, will not allow them to fully utilize their economic and military power in their war of aggression.” In other words, Hanoi ignored the intended signal from Washington and refused to back down.

Other pitfalls are associated with demonstrating resolve by standing firm and fighting. At the extreme, a state that leans too much on military strength can gain a reputation for aggression in ways that undermine long-term deterrence. Napoleonic France and Nazi Germany both had strong reputations for resolve, but their offensive actions ultimately provoked other countries rather than deterring them. There is certainly a risk that U.S. actions under the second Trump administration—such as decapitating the Iranian government and threatening to annex Canada, Gaza, and Greenland—could similarly backfire. For example, there is growing evidence that U.S. allies and partners in Europe, the Middle East, and Asia are rethinking their reliance on the United States and are seeking other sources of security. Far from conceding to Trump’s demands, Canadian Prime Minister Mark Carney has described a “rupture” in relations between Washington and Ottawa and advocated for middle powers to band together to protect their sovereignty.

Even if using force bolsters a country’s reputation for resolve, it may harm the country’s broader credibility. For instance, Russia’s 2022 invasion of Ukraine and its willingness to absorb hundreds of thousands of casualties since then has sent a strong signal about its resolve. Yet its dramatic underperformance on the battlefield against a far smaller and less well-equipped army has likely diminished the international perception of its military effectiveness. The war in Iran could similarly tarnish the United States’ military reputation. Since February, Washington has depleted at least half of its precision-strike missiles and advanced air defense interceptor missiles. It will likely take years to replenish these stockpiles given slow manufacturing times for so-called exquisite platforms. In the meantime, the United States has fewer advanced systems to draw on if China makes a move against Taiwan or if Russia attacks Poland or Estonia. For both Russia’s and the United States’ military campaigns, then, the net effect has been to reduce rather than enhance the countries’ reputation for military capability in the eyes of their respective adversaries.

Quite apart from the negative consequences that often come with decisions to use force, there is an even more fundamental problem with conventional thinking about credibility. In practice, backing down can actually be more helpful to a country’s reputation for resolve than fighting. This becomes especially clear in looking at the long-term effects of engaging in military interventions. Although the United States was victorious in World War I, the conflict produced a lingering war-weariness among the U.S. public and key policymakers. In the years after the Armistice, Americans expressed strong support for the idea that the United States should stay out of any future European war, a sentiment that was ultimately codified in the Neutrality Acts of the 1930s. Only after Japan attacked Pearl Harbor in December 1941, more than two years after war began in Europe, did the United States enter World War II. Fighting in World War I sent a signal of strong resolve to adversaries, but Americans’ war-weariness and resurgent isolationism in the years that followed conveyed that the United States would be less resolved to fight.

This dynamic has been even more apparent in the aftermath of major U.S. wars since then. Following the Vietnam War, President Jimmy Carter declared that “young Americans should never again be sent to die in battle unless our own nation is endangered.” Consider how this kind of thinking affected adversaries’ view of the United States: In deciding to invade Kuwait in 1990, Iraqi dictator Saddam Hussein assumed that the United States was intensely casualty averse and might not intervene or would back down quickly. General Wafiq al-Samarrai, Iraq’s head of military intelligence during the Gulf War, said in a 1996 interview that Saddam “thought that America’s involvement in Vietnam had badly damaged its willingness to use military power.” Although the Vietnam War was primarily fought to buttress the United States’ reputation for resolve, it had the opposite effect in practice.

And again, during and after the U.S. war in Iraq, Americans became skeptical of costly military interventions. As a result, recent administrations have been especially hesitant to commit ground troops in combat operations. Iran is undoubtedly aware of this so-called Iraq syndrome, which may embolden Tehran to act more aggressively without having to fear a large-scale American ground operation.

There is also experimental evidence that backing down may strengthen a country’s long-term reputation for resolve. In a survey I conducted in 2021 with 100 British parliamentarians, respondents were randomly divided into two groups. The first group read about a situation in which a hypothetical country backed down from a prior conflict. The second group read about a hypothetical country that stood firm in a prior conflict but displayed the same signs of war-weariness or “syndromes” that emerged in the United States after the Vietnam and Iraq wars. Both groups were then asked to estimate the likelihood that the country in question would stand firm or back down in a current dispute with the United Kingdom. On average, parliamentarians believed that the country that backed down in the prior dispute would have greater resolve in the current dispute. In other words, if fighting leads to war-weariness, then backing down can actually be better for a state’s future credibility.

As the U.S. campaign in Iran has demonstrated, choosing to fight to maintain a reputation for resolve is an incredibly risky gamble and may backfire. To avoid repeating the same mistake, U.S. policymakers and strategists need to rethink the relationship between credibility and fighting. First, the United States should avoid fighting altogether when the primary objective is shoring up reputation rather than defending a core national issue. If a conflict directly involves key security and economic interests, as did the U.S. Revolutionary War and World War II, then fighting can be a logical choice, assuming the United States has sufficient military capabilities to win the war at reasonable cost. But if the primary argument for the war relates to future credibility, as in Vietnam, then policymakers should be wary.

Second, if Washington determines that an adversary must be confronted with military force, then it must focus on limited, achievable objectives. Wars that drag on because they have much larger aims, or are prolonged simply for reputational reasons, may erode the country’s credibility by draining munitions and making Americans more reluctant to fight in the future. The two U.S. interventions against Saddam Hussein’s Iraq illustrate this point well. In the limited 1991 Gulf War, the United States quickly expelled Iraqi forces from Kuwait at low cost and before war-weariness could set in among the U.S. public. But the U.S.-led invasion of Iraq in 2003, in which Washington pursued the far more expansive goal of regime change, continued for years at high cost and made many U.S. policymakers and ordinary citizens alike less supportive of using force in the future.

Finally, policymakers should focus less on the U.S. reputation for resolve, which they cannot control, and more on the country’s military capabilities, which they can. To deter Beijing and Moscow, Washington needs to convince both adversaries that it has the ability to prosecute a modern great-power war. The problem is that, although the United States continues to have the most advanced military capabilities and weapons systems in the world, they are limited in quantity, extremely expensive, and take a long time to replace. In the course of just a few months of fighting against Iran, the United States depleted huge percentages of its advanced and expensive stockpiles of offensive and defensive missiles.

This alarming shortfall raises new questions about whether the United States could sustain a longer war of attrition of the type Russia and Ukraine are fighting. Washington urgently needs to replenish its stockpiles and rethink its dependence on a small number of exquisite systems. It also needs to avoid costly conflicts that distract it from the principal threats it faces from China and Russia. Rather than making U.S. credibility stronger in the eyes of the world, continuing the war in Iran could well undermine it, just as fighting in Vietnam and Iraq did.

How Arab Parties Can Rescue Israeli Democracy

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Since its founding in 1922, Foreign Affairs has been the leading forum for serious discussion of American foreign policy and global affairs. The magazine has featured contributions from many leading international affairs experts.

AYMAN ODEH served in the Knesset from 2015 to 2026. He is the former leader of the Joint List and the Hadash party.

As the Israeli opposition leader Gadi Eisenkot struggles to assemble a governing coalition following last month’s elections, Israel finds itself in a familiar political predicament. The parties opposed to Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu and his far-right coalition command a clear majority in the Knesset, with 67 of its 120 seats. Yet the four Jewish-led parties in Eisenkot’s camp hold just 48 seats, leaving them unable to form a government on their own. The impasse echoes the prolonged political deadlock between 2019 and 2021, when Netanyahu’s supporters and his opponents remained locked in competing parliamentary blocs, and four consecutive elections failed to produce a stable governing majority.

But one thing is different this time. Israel’s Arab-led parties, the Joint List and Raam, are stronger than ever. Powered by extraordinary turnout among Arab citizens of Israel, they won a record 19 seats in the Knesset: 11 for the Joint List and eight for Raam. This political mobilization is important in itself, demonstrating Arab voters’ overwhelming desire for change and their recognition of the importance of participating in elections. But its significance goes far beyond the number of seats they have won. Together, the Arab parties now have the potential to be a decisive force in an anti-Netanyahu coalition, providing the parliamentary backing needed to form a government. The proof of this power has already been shown: on November 17, Arab support was crucial in the vote to replace the Likud speaker of the Knesset with a speaker from Eisenkot’s party. Now they have a historic opportunity to put the country on a different path.

As the first and former leader of the Joint List, I know how much is at stake. In 2019, I helped lead a groundbreaking effort to give Arab-led parties a direct voice in the Israeli political system. We took an enormous political risk by endorsing Benny Gantz, then a former chief of staff of the Israeli military and Netanyahu’s principal rival, as a candidate to form a government. We did so despite our profound political and ideological differences with him. Although that effort ultimately fell short, with Gantz choosing to pursue a “unity government” with Netanyahu instead, it established an important precedent: by showing a willingness to work with Jewish political forces with whom they had deep disagreements, parties representing the Arab public had a real chance to prevent a Netanyahu-led far-right government from holding power.

Today, after nearly four years of disastrous leadership by Netanyahu and his far-right allies, that possibility has become far more urgent. It will take political courage on both sides. Eisenkot must be able to offer something to the Arab-led parties, and the Arab-led parties will need to be comfortable offering support to a government that they may not agree with on many issues. But the remarkable Arab turnout and the sizable Israeli majority that is now against Netanyahu have pointed to a way forward. Not only Israel’s future but also its place in the region and the world may depend on the willingness of its leaders to engage with the country’s Palestinian minority. Ultimately, such a partnership will be essential to building the trust between Jews and Arabs that is a prerequisite to achieving any real progress toward a viable Israeli-Palestinian peace. This is the real choice now facing Israel: whether Israelis are prepared to replace the politics of supremacy and exclusion with a politics of equality.

The factors that drove record numbers of Palestinian citizens of Israel to the polls in October have been years in the making. Although the country’s Arab citizens make up more than 20 percent of the Israeli electorate and have long made vital contributions to Israeli society as doctors, lawyers, engineers, high-tech professionals, and in many other fields, the Israeli right has for decades demonized them as enemies: a fifth column and a threat to the state. In recent years, this hostility has increasingly manifested itself in harassment and open racism at universities and workplaces, in the media, in the parliament, and in everyday life.

Yet it would be a mistake to assume that Arab exclusion has been driven only by Netanyahu and the radical right. Zionist parties of the center and the left have also contributed, by keeping a distance from Arab voters or simply ignoring them. The belief that Jewish citizens should ultimately determine the country’s political direction while Arab citizens must remain within whatever limits have been set for them runs deep in Israeli politics. Netanyahu understood early on how to turn this contradiction into a political strategy. Since the 1990s, he has repeatedly used the same formula: inciting his supporters against Arab citizens, delegitimizing Arab representatives in parliament, and convincing Jewish Israelis that a government that relies on Arab political support would somehow be illegitimate.

These tactics proved to be among Netanyahu’s most effective political tools, especially given that Arab-led parties have long been some of his biggest foes. He didn’t need to convince everyone to support his Likud party or its various coalition partners. He only needed to convince enough people, including Jewish Israelis who opposed him, that working with Arab-led parties was dangerous and unacceptable. Netanyahu’s strategy also helped suppress Arab turnout. Since the Arab-led parties had little chance of changing the status quo, Arab voters had little incentive to vote: for many years, a significantly lower proportion of Arab citizens went to the polls than did their Jewish counterparts.

That began to change in 2015 with the creation of the Joint List, which for the first time brought together four parties representing the Arab public on a single electoral ticket and which became the third-largest faction in the Knesset. By embracing a broader vision for our society, grounded in Jewish-Arab solidarity and opposition to the racism of the right, the Joint List demonstrated that Palestinian citizens of Israel could play a new and more influential role in the country’s political system. Then, under my leadership in 2019, the Joint List decided to back Gantz’s bid to unseat Netanyahu. The decision was highly controversial, both within the Arab electorate and across Israeli society. Many Arab voters were deeply ambivalent about supporting a potential government led by a former army chief whose political positions, particularly on the status of Palestinian citizens of Israel and the occupation, differed sharply from their own. Nevertheless, we were prepared to take that political risk because we understood that defeating Netanyahu was more important.

In practice, our willingness to cooperate with the Zionist opposition was not, in fact, reciprocated. The Jewish opposition parties wanted our votes but could not bring themselves to accept us as equal political partners. And, ultimately, Gantz decided to form a government with Netanyahu instead. Nevertheless, the experience showed that Jewish-Arab political cooperation could open up new horizons for a large segment of the public. Since then, every time that Arab-led parties have recommended or supported the effort of a Jewish candidate to form a government, or whenever the Israeli center-left has depended on their support, additional segments of Israeli society have become more open to this kind of cooperation.

In 2021–22, an Arab party, Raam, joined the short-lived government led by Naftali Bennett and Yair Lapid. I had serious reservations about the model of partnership that emerged, which seemed to rest on the expectation that Arab political representatives would limit their involvement to civilian and domestic matters, while refraining from addressing political and national questions, particularly the occupation and the Palestinian issue. I reject this distinction. We are part of the Palestinian people, and our identity and political concerns cannot be separated from our citizenship in Israel.

But these kinds of slow and painful steps are how political change often happens in national struggles. It does not always begin with a moral or democratic awakening. Sometimes, it begins with pragmatic necessity. When there is no other option, people begin to reconsider what had once been seen as impossible. In the current election, that dynamic has become even more important. After the unending crisis of the past four years, many Israeli voters, including both Arabs and Jews as well as the leaders of most of the Zionist opposition parties, have concluded that getting rid of the Netanyahu government is more necessary than ever.

In recent past elections, voter turnout among Palestinian citizens of Israel has generally remained between 50 and 60 percent. What explains the unprecedented 20-percentage-point surge in turnout in the October election? Three developments stand out: the war of annihilation in Gaza and the killing of tens of thousands of Palestinians; the daily racism and hostility that Arab citizens now face in the streets, in public spaces, and in virtually every aspect of their lives; and the crime that has spiraled out of control in Arab communities under the national security minister, Itamar Ben-Gvir, whose policies have allowed it to flourish, and the relentless settler violence against Palestinians in the West Bank enabled by Finance Minister Bezalel Smotrich. All the grief and frustration accumulated over these years were brought to the ballot box.

But there was another crucial factor as well. In the run-up to the election, Arab citizens, following the nearly even polls between Eisenkot and Netanyahu, could see in black and white just how much the outcome would depend on them. Voting this time would be more than a political choice. It was for many a personal responsibility and a way of taking control of their own future. That is why hundreds of thousands of Palestinian citizens of Israel made their way to the polls, defying years of efforts to silence them and undermine their political agency. Many understood that the future direction of the country might be quite literally in their hands.

I saw elderly people, like my friend Jumah from Jisr al-Zarqa, taking to the streets and campaigning for the Joint List. Many of them had lived through the 1948 nakba, or “catastrophe,” which accompanied Israel’s establishment and involved terrible massacres, the destruction of hundreds of Palestinian villages and towns, and the displacement of around 750,000 Palestinians. I saw young people debating politics in the cafes and restaurants of my beloved Haifa, persuading their friends to vote. I saw women and men from Nazareth, Umm al-Fahm, Sakhnin, Rahat, Akko, Kafr Yasif, Majd al-Krum, and countless other towns and villages united by a shared sense of purpose. I saw political consciousness become a living, collective force. For a moment, the boundaries between generations and communities seemed to disappear.

The result marks a remarkable advance of the process of Arab participation in the Israeli political system that began in 2019. In this election, Arab citizens of Israel turned out in record numbers, dramatically narrowing the historical turnout gap with Jewish voters. And that has made another political reality increasingly clear: Israel’s liberal and centrist camp cannot form a government without the parties that represent the Arab public. If it excludes them, as well as the ultra-Orthodox and the far right, it will not reach the 61 seats needed. This is the demographic reality. Israeli Jews who oppose Netanyahu must decide whom they are willing to build a political future with.

The debate about what Eisenkot should do and whether he should ask for Arab support is now unfolding beyond the Knesset, in people’s homes, cafés, in universities, and on the street. It is a deeply controversial question, and many Israelis disagree strenuously about it. Yet for the Arab-led parties, gaining sufficient importance to be able to provoke these conversations is an achievement. The fact that this debate is even taking place shows how the political process has caused Israelis to consider possibilities that until recently seemed unimaginable.

In the end, Eisenkot may have few other options if he wants to replace Netanyahu, as he has promised. In most cases, political parties support coalitions to advance positive changes and achieve concrete goals. But there are moments when political cooperation is necessary for a different reason: to prevent something far worse. The most immediate and overriding objective today is to bring an end to the far-right Netanyahu government. That is a priority on which both the Zionist opposition and the Arab-led parties can agree.

Beyond this shared basic goal, the Arab-led parties should use their political influence to work on two levels. First, they must seek tangible improvements in their constituents’ lives: combating organized crime in Arab communities, securing budgets, addressing the housing crisis, ending the brutal treatment of Palestinians living under occupation in the West Bank, and pushing for a permanent cease-fire and the reconstruction of Gaza. These are fundamental issues of human dignity and civilian life. On a broader level, they should continue to push for real progress on Israeli-Palestinian peace that is ultimately based on two states coexisting, side by side.

I do not expect the current Israeli political establishment to embrace these positions, but that does not mean that the Arab parties should not raise them at every opportunity. At the same time, the Arab parties can use their newfound political clout to build alliances between Jews and Arabs around shared political and strategic goals. The resulting partnerships must be about more than parliamentary arrangements; it must also create the foundations for a different future. That will require Jewish and Arab citizens of Israel to engage with one another on the basis of equality, rather than mere political convenience.

Thus far, Eisenkot has given few indications that he is prepared to go down this path. Since the election, it has been encouraging to see him condemn Netanyahu for fueling what he described as a “racist and violent” campaign against Israel’s Arab citizens. Yet many of the mainstream Zionist parties in Eisenkot’s camp have vowed not to work with the Arab parties. Still, Eisenkot must consider what that would mean. Thirty years from now, people will ask how Jewish supremacy became so normalized that even politicians who called themselves democrats accepted the exclusion of one-fifth of their fellow citizens. They will ask why Israel’s opposition could recognize the danger posed by Netanyahu’s government but not confront the assumptions that made his politics possible.

There is an irony in the fact that, in a country that seeks to keep political hegemony exclusively in Jewish hands, the Palestinian minority, after decades of struggle for political recognition and equal rights, has emerged as one of the most significant forces shaping the country’s political future. We have come to understand that we must be courageous and cooperate with the Zionist parties of the center and left in order to oust Netanyahu, Ben-Gvir, and Smotrich. The question now is: Do these parties, which call themselves democratic and liberal, have the same courage?

Fintech startup Payhawk valued at $2B in $280M round

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Payhawk Ltd., a startup that helps enterprises manage their business expenses, has raised $280 million in funding.

The Series C round was led by Goldman Sachs’ growth equity arm. The bank was joined by Greenoaks, Lightspeed and more than a half-dozen others. Payhawk stated today that it’s now valued at $2 billion.

Issuing corporate credit cards to employees historically involved a weeks-long wait. Payhawk operates a platform that enables companies to create virtual cards in a few seconds and issue physical ones in five days. According to the company, customers can configure a card to expire automatically at the end of the project for which it’s needed.

Accounting teams can set spending limits to avoid cost overruns. Payhawk makes it possible to tie purchase caps to a business project, a specific merchant or a broad product category.

Companies can optionally connect their cards to Payhawk-operated business accounts. They support payments in more than 115 currencies. According to the software maker, its accounts’ currency exchange rates are up to 90% lower than those charged by traditional banks.

Syncing corporate card spending to a company’s accounting ledger is a multi-step process. First, workers must scan the receipt issued in connection with a purchase and send it to the finance team. From there, an accountant categorizes the purchase and verifies it through a process known as reconciliation.

Payhawk automates the workflow using an artificial intelligence agent. Additionally, it flags cost-cutting opportunities such as unnecessary software-as-a-service subscriptions.

A second AI agent speeds up procurement initiatives. It’s accessible through a chat interface that enables workers to request items in natural language. If a request is missing certain details, the agent can point out what additional information is needed. From there, it automatically seeks approvals from relevant executives.

Payhawk also automates a number of other tasks. It includes a tool that reduces the amount of work involved in changing business trip plans. Additionally, the company has built an AI engine that enables new users to customize its platform by uploading documents such as spending policies.

“Through our technology, finance departments can benefit from ‘zero-touch finance’, where neither the finance professional nor the employee deals with any admin work,” said Payhawk co-founder and Chief Executive Officer Hristo Borisov. “77% of our customers use our agents daily across financial control, procurement, travel and reporting to save time and money for their businesses.”

Payhawk says that it still has 70% of its previous funding round, a $100 million investment that closed in 2022. The company will use the proceeds from today’s Series C raise to enhance its AI capabilities.

Scale the Model, Scale the Harness: Unlocking a 2 Leap in Mathematical Reasoning

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Originally published in Generative AI on Medium.

I spent months building a system that wraps a language model in a checking loop so it can prove hard math theorems. Every step gets verified with real math tools, the system remembers what worked, and a second pass tries to break the proof before it counts.

Then I tested it on 300 hard math problems, and the results surprised me: the setup around the model matters as much as the model itself.

The honest headline is the last column: the loop fixed 16–33% of the problems each model got wrong on its own. Flash is the best model and improves least in multiples (1.64x, not 2x) but most in failures rescued.

The detail I'm proudest of: the local 27B catches factor-of-2 and boundary-case errors both big models miss. Five problems only Qwen solved; it beat Muse on 14 problems and Flash on 10. Flash is also 7.8x faster than Muse at the median solve (12.2 vs 95.0 minutes).

Proofs are graded by our own calibrated LLM judge ensemble, running in an isolated context — no human grading, no formal verification. All the eval logs are public so anyone can recount: certified counts reproduce exactly from the judgment files (Qwen 80, Muse 139, Flash 166).

The full article is published in Generative AI on Medium: https://generativeai.pub/scale-the-model-scale-the-harness-unlocking-a-2-leap-in-mathematical-reasoning-231314ab3ea7

The code and all test results are open: https://github.com/dionyziuz/proof_harness — eval logs: https://github.com/dionyziuz/proof_harness/tree/main/data/eval_results

If you try it or spot a flaw in the method, I'd like to hear it — the comments here or an issue on the repo.

MegaRobo Raises Nearly US$200 Million in New Funding to Build Intelligence Infrastructure for the Physical World

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

AI's Next Frontier Is the Physical World. Science Is Where It Begins.

SUZHOU, China, Nov. 18, 2026 /PRNewswire/ -- MegaRobo Technologies (MegaRobo), a technology company building Intelligence Infrastructure for the Physical World, today announced that it has raised nearly US$200 million in a new financing round joined by Oman Investment Authority (OIA), the sovereign wealth fund of the Sultanate of Oman. And existing investors Asia Investment Capital, Granite Asia and Sinovation Ventures continued to back the company, with new participation from CMB International.

The new financing will support MegaRobo's continued investment in core technology, the global expansion of its AI for Science business, and the development of infrastructure that enables AI to operate in increasingly complex physical environments.

The first generation of AI learned primarily from digital information - text, images, video and code. The next generation will increasingly learn by interacting with the physical world: acting on real systems, observing outcomes, and using those outcomes to decide what to do next.

In science, that means moving beyond reading papers, predicting structures or generating molecules. AI must also be able to design experiments, execute them in real laboratories, observe the results and learn from what happens.

MegaRobo is building the infrastructure for that transition. Its proprietary One Intelligence Stack connects intelligence with execution - bringing together models and agents, data, workflows, software, scientific instruments, robotics, automation and real-world feedback in a single reusable technology foundation.

One Intelligence Stack. Across Complex Physical Systems.THINK  â  DESIGN  â  EXECUTE  â  OBSERVE  â  LEARN

Science is the first proving ground for this architecture because it requires AI to do more than analyze information. Scientific discovery depends on an ongoing loop between reasoning, design, physical experimentation, observation and learning - often under conditions where the answer is not known in advance.

MegaRobo is applying this architecture to scientific discovery and pharmaceutical R&D through an integrated AI for Science infrastructure that combines AI, scientific computing, experimental execution, data and R&D decision-making.

The company has delivered products and technologies to more than 500 customers, including leading global pharmaceutical companies, drug discovery organizations, biotechnology companies and research institutions. Its systems support key stages of discovery and development, including early research, target and mechanism discovery, drug screening and candidate optimization.

Through Megalaxy Lab, MegaRobo is also making real-world experimentation increasingly programmable. Researchers and AI agents can configure experiments, route them to automated laboratory systems, track execution, and receive structured, machine-readable results that can feed directly into the next cycle of scientific reasoning. Megalaxy Lab has served more than 200 customers.

MegaRobo is now extending this capability to the global AI for Science community. Together with industry partners including Tencent Cloud and Twist Bioscience, the company has launched Poreva Open, a global AI protein design challenge open to all models and technical approaches. Megalaxy Lab will provide wet-lab validation for more than 10,000 protein design sequences, creating an opportunity for innovators worldwide to test their models against real experimental results. By opening up large-scale experimental validation to the global research community, Poreva Open aims to help establish a common benchmark for AI-driven protein design.

This marks a shift in how scientific infrastructure is accessed: from laboratory work organized mainly as individual projects toward experimental capacity that can increasingly be standardized, automated and called on demand by scientists, software and AI agents.

A Reusable Foundation for Complex Physical Systems

Science is where MegaRobo is starting, but the underlying architecture is designed for other complex physical systems as well. Wherever AI must interact with instruments, machines, materials, processes and real-world feedback, the same core challenge appears: intelligence must connect to execution, and execution must generate observations that intelligence can learn from.

MegaRobo is developing One Intelligence Stack as a common technology foundation for this interaction - not as a collection of isolated AI applications or automation products, but as infrastructure that enables AI to act, observe and learn across complex physical environments.

Daniel Huang, Founder and CEO of MegaRobo, said: "The first generation of AI learned from humanity's accumulated knowledge. The next generation will increasingly learn by acting in the physical world - through experiments, production and continuous feedback. That shift changes AI from a system that interprets the world into one that can help create new knowledge, materials, processes and products. MegaRobo is building the infrastructure for that transition."

Liu Erh Fei, Founding Partner and CEO of Asia Investment Capital, said:"As a long-term shareholder, we have seen MegaRobo continuously drive innovation, earn the trust of its customers and translate its technological capabilities into commercial applications. Its ability to integrate AI, software and hardware, together with its experience in scientific research and advanced manufacturing, underpins our continued investment. We look forward to continuing to support MegaRobo as it further strengthens its product and technology platforms and serves more customers worldwide."

The new round builds on MegaRobo's long-standing backing from leading global investors and strategic partners. To date, the company has raised approximately US$600 million across multiple financing rounds. This includes a nearly US$300 million Series C round co-led by Goldman Sachs Alternatives, Asia Investment Capital and Granite Asia, with participation from Pavilion Capital, a part of Temasek's Seviora Group. Long-term investors such as Sinovation Ventures, Joy Capital and MPCi have continued to support MegaRobo through multiple stages of its growth. Earlier industry investors also include WuXi AppTec and Bosch Ventures.

Together, this diverse group of technology, financial and industrial partners reflect MegaRobo's evolution into a global technology company with deep capabilities spanning scientific discovery and complex physical systems. Its continued expansion across North America, Europe, the Middle East, and Asia will accelerate the global scale-up of intelligence infrastructure for the physical world.
